It is now strictly an attribute of the method being _called_ , declaring itself to be inline and which arguments are inline.\\n```\\n... -> (int, bool) /* b, c */\\n\\n```\\n\\nReturn values are no longer named in [method declarations](language.html#methods-detailed), rather only their types need to be declared. This also means they are not inherently available as variables in the method body scope.\\n```\\nlocal int b;\\nlocal bool c;\\n\\n```\\n\\nPreviously, these variables were declared as return values. Built-in templates can be categorized as follows:\\n  * Each object type has a corresponding template which is instantiated for all objects of that type. For instance, the template `register` is automatically instantiated in all registers. All such templates inherit the `object` template, and define the `objtype` parameter to the name of the object type, e.g. `\\"register\\"` for registers.\\n  * Some templates primarily provide a standard implementation of some behaviour. For instance, the `uint64_attr` template can be applied on `attribute` objects to make it a simple integer attribute.\\n  * Some templates primarily specify a programming interface, typically by providing an abstract or overrideable method or parameter. Such templates are often named like the provided member. For instance, objects that implement the `init` template provide the abstract method `init`. Interface templates have a number of uses:\\n    * In some cases, an interface template extends an existing template, altering its default behaviour to make sure its interface method is called. Often, this means that when you provide an implementation of an interface method, you _must_ instantiate the corresponding template; otherwise, the method will not be called. For instance, if you implement the `write` method in a register, it is not called by default upon a write access; however, if you instantiate the `write` template in the register, then the register\'s behaviour is altered to call the `write` method. Thus, in order to provide custom side-effects on a write, you must write something like:\\n```\\nregister r @ 0 is write { method write(uint64 value) { default();\\nlog info: \\"wrote r\\"; } }\\n\\n```\\n\\n    * When writing a method or parameter override _inside a template_ , you must explicitly instantiate the template you are overriding in order to specify that your declaration takes precedence over the default implementation. If your template is intended for a specific object type, then it is sufficient to override that template, but it is often better to override a more specific template if possible. For instance, the `init_val` parameter belongs to the `init_val` template, which is inherited by all registers. So a template that overrides this parameter may be implemented as follows:\\n```\\ntemplate init_to_ten is register {\\n  param init_val = 10;\\n}\\n\\n```\\n\\nHowever, it is even better to only inherit the `init_val` template:\\n```\\ntemplate init_to_ten is init_val {\\n  param init_val = 10;\\n}\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe latter improves efficiency and permits `init_to_ten` to also be used on fields.\\n    * Similarly, if you write a template that needs to _access_ some member of the object, then it must inherit a template that provides that member. For instance:\\n```\\ntemplate log_on_change is (write, get, name) {\\n  method write(uint64 value) {\\n    if (this.get() != value) {\\n      log info: \\"%s changed!\\", this.name;\\n    }\\n    default();\\n  }\\n}",\n      "metadata": {\n        "url": "https://intel.github.io/simics/docs/dml-1.4-reference-manual/dml-builtins.html",\n        "source": "intel.github.io",\n        "headers": "# [4 Libraries and Built-ins](#libraries-and-built-ins)",\n        "source_id": "intel.github.io",\n        "char_count": 3213,\n        "chunk_size": 3213,\n        "local_file": "pipeline_output/downloaded_pages/simics_docs_dml-1.4-reference-manual_dml-builtins.html",\n        "word_count": 488,\n        "chunk_index": 0\n      },\n      "similarity": "NaN",\n      "source_id": "intel.github.io",\n      "rerank_score": 5.970747947692871\n    },\n    {\n      "url": "https://intel.github.io/simics/docs/dml-1.4-reference-manual/dml-builtins.html",\n      "content": "```\\n\\nAgain, it would also work to inherit `register` instead of `get` and `name`, but at the cost of reduced flexibility and efficiency.\\n\\n\\n## [4.1 Universal templates](#universal-templates)\\nThe following templates are applicable to all object kinds:\\n### [4.1.1 name](#name)\\nProvides a string parameter `name`, containing the name of the object, as exposed to the end-user. This parameter is typically used in log messages and names of configuration attributes. The name can be overridden in order to hide confidential information from the end-user.\\n### [4.1.2 desc](#desc)\\nProvides a string parameter `desc`, with a short description in plain text. By convention, this should preferably be a few descriptive words, but may also be a long name. The description can appear during simulation, when inspecting the device, and also serves as a docstring that enriches the DML source code. The parameter\'s default value is `undefined`. The `desc` parameter has a short-hand syntax described in section [Object Declarations](language.html#object-declarations).\\nAlso provides a string parameter `shown_desc`, which is the string actually exposed to the end-user during simulation. This parameter defaults to `desc`, and can be overridden in order to hide confidential information from the end-user.\\nSee also [template `documentation`](#documentation).\\n### [4.1.3 shown_desc](#shown_desc)\\nA subtemplate of [`desc`](#desc) that makes the `shown_desc` parameter a typed parameter. This is inherited by objects that need to access `shown_desc` from the context of a shared method.\\n### [4.1.4 documentation](#documentation)\\nProvides a string parameter `documentation`, with a longer description. The documentation may appear when extracting documentation from the device.\\nIf you have the _Documentation and Packaging_ package and intend to generate Simics reference documentation for the device then the `documentation` string must follow the Simics documentation XML format, otherwise you will get a syntax error during the documentation build. See the _Writing Documentation_ application note.\\nAlso provides a string parameter `shown_documentation`, defaulting to `documentation`. This parameter is similar to `shown_desc` in the [desc](#desc) template, and is mainly a convenient way to suppress documentation.\\n### [4.1.5 limitations](#limitations)\\nProvides a string parameter `limitations`, describing limitations in the implementation of this object. The documentation may appear when extracting documentation from the device.\\nIf you have the _Documentation and Packaging_ package and intend to generate Simics reference documentation for the device then the `limitations` string must follow the Simics documentation XML format, otherwise you will get a syntax error during the documentation build. See the _Writing Documentation_ application note.\\nAlso provides a string parameter `shown_limitations`, defaulting to `limitations`. This parameter is similar to `shown_desc`, and is mainly a convenient way to suppress documentation.\\n### [4.1.6 init](#init)\\nProvides an abstract method `init`, which is called when the device is created, _before_ any attributes have been initialized. Typically used to initialize a default value, or to set up data structures.\\nThe method `init` is automatically called on all objects that implement the `init` template. The order in which `init` of objects are called is not defined, except that `init` of a particular object is guaranteed to be called before `init` of any of its parent objects. In particular, `init` of the device object will be called only after all other implementations of `init`.\\n### [4.1.7 post_init](#post_init)\\nProvides an abstract method `post_init`, which is called when the device is created, _after_ any attributes have been initialized. Typically used to establish connections to other devices, or to set up data structures that depend on configured attribute values.\\nThe method `post_init` is automatically called on all objects that implement the `post_init` template.\\n### [4.1.8 destroy](#destroy)\\nProvides an abstract method `destroy`, which is called when the device is being deleted. This provides a means to clean up resources associated with the device instance that are not managed by DMLC (and `destroy` should not be used for any other purpose). This typically amounts to invoking [`delete`](language.html#delete-statements) on any device state that has been dynamically allocated using [`new`](language.html#new-expressions).\\nThe method `destroy` is automatically called on all objects that implement the `destroy` template. The order in which `destroy` of objects are called is not defined, except that `destroy` of a particular object is guaranteed to be called before `destroy` of any of its parent objects. In particular, `destroy` of the device object will be called only after all other implementations of `destroy`.\\nUsage notes:\\n  * While the device is being deleted, it is not allowed to communicate with any other Simics ob",\n      "metadata": {\n        "url": "https://intel.github.io/simics/docs/dml-1.4-reference-manual/dml-builtins.html",\n        "source": "intel.github.io",\n        "headers": "## [4.1 Universal templates](#universal-templates); ### [4.1.1 name](#name); ### [4.1.2 desc](#desc); ### [4.1.3 shown_desc](#shown_desc); ### [4.1.4 documentation](#documentation); ### [4.1.5 limitations](#limitations); ### [4.1.6 init](#init); ### [4.1.7 post_init](#post_init); ### [4.1.8 destroy](#destroy)",\n        "source_id": "intel.github.io",\n        "char_count": 5000,\n        "chunk_size": 5000,\n        "local_file": "pipeline_output/downloaded_pages/simics_docs_dml-1.4-reference-manual_dml-builtins.html",\n        "word_count": 717,\n        "chunk_index": 1\n      },\n      "similarity": "NaN",\n      "source_id": "intel.github.io",\n      "rerank_score": -2.1832122802734375\n    },\n    {\n      "url": "https://intel.github.io/simics/docs/dml-1.4-reference-manual/dml-builtins.html",\n      "content": "ject (whether that is by accessing `connect`ed devices or by leveraging the Simics API.) Inherits the templates [`name`](#name), [`desc`](#desc), [`documentation`](#documentation) and [`limitations`](#limitations). Provides the following additional parameters, which cannot be overridden:\\n  * `this` (reference): Always refers to the current object, i.e., the nearest enclosing object definition.\\n  * `objtype`: string constant describing the object type, e.g. `\\"register\\"`\\n  * `parent` (reference or `undefined`): Always refers to the parent (containing) object. Has the value `undefined` in the `device` object.\\n  * qname: The fully qualified name, including indices, such as `some_bank.r0`. Constructed from the `name` parameter. In the device object, this is equal to the `name` parameter.\\n  * dev: The top-level `device` object\\n  * templates: see [Template-Qualified Method Implementation Calls](language.html#template-qualified-method-implementation-calls).\\n  * indices: List of local indices for this object. For a non-array object, this is the empty list. In a register array of size N, it is a list with one element, a non-negative integer smaller than N. The parameter is _not_ cumulative across the object hierarchy, so for a single field inside a register array, the value is the empty list.\\n  * Each array has an _individual index parameter_ , to make it possible to refer to both inner and outer indexes when arrays are nested (cf. the `indices` parameter, above). The parameter name is specified in the array declaration; for instance, the declaration `register regs[i < 4][j < 11];` defines two index parameters, `i` and `j`. In this case, the `indices` parameter is `[i, j]`.\\n\\n\\nThe `object` template provides the non-overridable method `cancel_after()`, which cancels all pending events posted using `after` which are associated with the object (any events associated with subobjects are unaffected).\\nThere are no other methods common for all objects, but the methods `init`, `post_init`, and `destroy` are automatically called on all objects that implement the [`init`](#init), [`post_init`](#post_init), and [`destroy`](#destroy) template, respectively.\\n## [4.2 Device objects](#device-objects)\\nThe top-level scope of a DML file defines the _device object_ , defined by the template `device`. The current state of the signal is stored in the saved boolean `high`, and a spec-violation message is logged on level 2 if the signal is raised or lowered when already high or low. The methods `signal_raise` and `signal_lower` can be overridden to add additional side effects.\\n### [5.5.2 signal_connect](#signal_connect)\\nImplements a connect with a signal interface, with saved state. The current state of the signal is stored in the saved boolean `signal.high`. If the connect is changed while `signal.high` is `true` and the device is configured, the `signal_lower` method will be called on the old object, and the `signal_raise` method will be called on the new object. Similarly, if the device is created with `signal.high` set to `true`, the `signal_raise` method will be called on the connected object in the finalize phase. This behaviour can be changed by overriding the `set` method and/or the `post_init` method. The template defines the following method:\\n  * `set_level(uint1 high)`: Sets the level of the signal, by calling `signal_raise` or `signal_lower`, as required. ...\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe syntax for [inlining a method](language.html#inline-methods) call has changed. It is now strictly an attribute of the method being _called_ , declaring itself to be inline and which arguments are inline.\\n```\\n... -> (int, bool) /* b, c */\\n\\n```\\n\\nReturn values are no longer named in [method declarations](language.html#methods-detailed), rather only their types need to be declared. This also means they are not inherently available as variables in the method body scope.\\n```\\nlocal int b;\\nlocal bool c;\\n\\n```\\n\\nPreviously, these variables were declared as return values. Built-in templates can be categorized as follows:\\n  * Each object type has a corresponding template which is instantiated for all objects of that type. For instance, the template `register` is automatically instantiated in all registers. All such templates inherit the `object` template, and define the `objtype` parameter to the name of the object type, e.g. `\\"register\\"` for registers.\\n  * Some templates primarily provide a standard implementation of some behaviour. For instance, the `uint64_attr` template can be applied on `attribute` objects to make it a simple integer attribute.\\n  * Some templates primarily specify a programming interface, typically by providing an abstract or overrideable method or parameter. Such templates are often named like the provided member. For instance, objects that implement the `init` template provide the abstract method `init`. Interface templates have a number of uses:\\n    * In some cases, an interface template extends an existing template, altering its default behaviour to make sure its interface method is called. Often, this means that when you provide an implementation of an interface method, you _must_ instantiate the corresponding template; otherwise, the method will not be called. For instance, if you implement the `write` method in a register, it is not called by default upon a write access; however, if you instantiate the `write` template in the register, then the register\'s behaviour is altered to call the `write` method. Thus, in order to provide custom side-effects on a write, you must write something like:\\n```\\nregister r @ 0 is write { method write(uint64 value) { default();\\nlog info: \\"wrote r\\"; } }\\n\\n```\\n\\n    * When writing a method or parameter override _inside a template_ , you must explicitly instantiate the template you are overriding in order to specify that your declaration takes precedence over the default implementation. If your template is intended for a specific object type, then it is sufficient to override that template, but it is often better to override a more specific template if possible. For instance, the `init_val` parameter belongs to the `init_val` template, which is inherited by all registers. So a template that overrides this parameter may be implemented as follows:\\n```\\ntemplate init_to_ten is register {\\n  param init_val = 10;\\n}\\n\\n```\\n\\nHowever, it is even better to only inherit the `init_val` template:\\n```\\ntemplate init_to_ten is init_val {\\n  param init_val = 10;\\n}\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe latter improves efficiency and permits `init_to_ten` to also be used on fields.\\n    * Similarly, if you write a template that needs to _access_ some member of the object, then it must inherit a template that provides that member. For instance:\\n```\\ntemplate log_on_change is (write, get, name) {\\n  method write(uint64 value) {\\n    if (this.get() != value) {\\n      log info: \\"%s changed!\\", this.name;\\n    }\\n    default();\\n  }\\n}",\n      "metadata": {\n        "url": "https://intel.github.io/simics/docs/dml-1.4-reference-manual/dml-builtins.html",\n        "source": "intel.github.io",\n        "headers": "# [4 Libraries and Built-ins](#libraries-and-built-ins)",\n        "source_id": "intel.github.io",\n        "char_count": 3213,\n        "chunk_size": 3213,\n        "local_file": "pipeline_output/downloaded_pages/simics_docs_dml-1.4-reference-manual_dml-builtins.html",\n        "word_count": 488,\n        "chunk_index": 0\n      },\n      "similarity": "NaN",\n      "source_id": "intel.github.io",\n      "rerank_score": 5.970747947692871\n    },\n    {\n      "url": "https://intel.github.io/simics/docs/dml-1.4-reference-manual/dml-builtins.html",\n      "content": "```\\n\\nAgain, it would also work to inherit `register` instead of `get` and `name`, but at the cost of reduced flexibility and efficiency.\\n\\n\\n## [4.1 Universal templates](#universal-templates)\\nThe following templates are applicable to all object kinds:\\n### [4.1.1 name](#name)\\nProvides a string parameter `name`, containing the name of the object, as exposed to the end-user. This parameter is typically used in log messages and names of configuration attributes. The name can be overridden in order to hide confidential information from the end-user.\\n### [4.1.2 desc](#desc)\\nProvides a string parameter `desc`, with a short description in plain text. By convention, this should preferably be a few descriptive words, but may also be a long name. The description can appear during simulation, when inspecting the device, and also serves as a docstring that enriches the DML source code. The parameter\'s default value is `undefined`. The `desc` parameter has a short-hand syntax described in section [Object Declarations](language.html#object-declarations).\\nAlso provides a string parameter `shown_desc`, which is the string actually exposed to the end-user during simulation. This parameter defaults to `desc`, and can be overridden in order to hide confidential information from the end-user.\\nSee also [template `documentation`](#documentation).\\n### [4.1.3 shown_desc](#shown_desc)\\nA subtemplate of [`desc`](#desc) that makes the `shown_desc` parameter a typed parameter. This is inherited by objects that need to access `shown_desc` from the context of a shared method.\\n### [4.1.4 documentation](#documentation)\\nProvides a string parameter `documentation`, with a longer description. The documentation may appear when extracting documentation from the device.\\nIf you have the _Documentation and Packaging_ package and intend to generate Simics reference documentation for the device then the `documentation` string must follow the Simics documentation XML format, otherwise you will get a syntax error during the documentation build. See the _Writing Documentation_ application note.\\nAlso provides a string parameter `shown_documentation`, defaulting to `documentation`. This parameter is similar to `shown_desc` in the [desc](#desc) template, and is mainly a convenient way to suppress documentation.\\n### [4.1.5 limitations](#limitations)\\nProvides a string parameter `limitations`, describing limitations in the implementation of this object. The documentation may appear when extracting documentation from the device.\\nIf you have the _Documentation and Packaging_ package and intend to generate Simics reference documentation for the device then the `limitations` string must follow the Simics documentation XML format, otherwise you will get a syntax error during the documentation build. See the _Writing Documentation_ application note.\\nAlso provides a string parameter `shown_limitations`, defaulting to `limitations`. This parameter is similar to `shown_desc`, and is mainly a convenient way to suppress documentation.\\n### [4.1.6 init](#init)\\nProvides an abstract method `init`, which is called when the device is created, _before_ any attributes have been initialized. Typically used to initialize a default value, or to set up data structures.\\nThe method `init` is automatically called on all objects that implement the `init` template. The order in which `init` of objects are called is not defined, except that `init` of a particular object is guaranteed to be called before `init` of any of its parent objects. In particular, `init` of the device object will be called only after all other implementations of `init`.\\n### [4.1.7 post_init](#post_init)\\nProvides an abstract method `post_init`, which is called when the device is created, _after_ any attributes have been initialized. Typically used to establish connections to other devices, or to set up data structures that depend on configured attribute values.\\nThe method `post_init` is automatically called on all objects that implement the `post_init` template.\\n### [4.1.8 destroy](#destroy)\\nProvides an abstract method `destroy`, which is called when the device is being deleted. This provides a means to clean up resources associated with the device instance that are not managed by DMLC (and `destroy` should not be used for any other purpose). This typically amounts to invoking [`delete`](language.html#delete-statements) on any device state that has been dynamically allocated using [`new`](language.html#new-expressions).\\nThe method `destroy` is automatically called on all objects that implement the `destroy` template. The order in which `destroy` of objects are called is not defined, except that `destroy` of a particular object is guaranteed to be called before `destroy` of any of its parent objects. Inherits the templates [`name`](#name), [`desc`](#desc), [`documentation`](#documentation) and [`limitations`](#limitations). Provides the following additional parameters, which cannot be overridden:\\n  * `this` (reference): Always refers to the current object, i.e., the nearest enclosing object definition.\\n  * `objtype`: string constant describing the object type, e.g. `\\"register\\"`\\n  * `parent` (reference or `undefined`): Always refers to the parent (containing) object. Has the value `undefined` in the `device` object.\\n  * qname: The fully qualified name, including indices, such as `some_bank.r0`. Constructed from the `name` parameter. In the device object, this is equal to the `name` parameter.\\n  * dev: The top-level `device` object\\n  * templates: see [Template-Qualified Method Implementation Calls](language.html#template-qualified-method-implementation-calls).\\n  * indices: List of local indices for this object. For a non-array object, this is the empty list. In a register array of size N, it is a list with one element, a non-negative integer smaller than N. The parameter is _not_ cumulative across the object hierarchy, so for a single field inside a register array, the value is the empty list.\\n  * Each array has an _individual index parameter_ , to make it possible to refer to both inner and outer indexes when arrays are nested (cf. the `indices` parameter, above). The parameter name is specified in the array declaration; for instance, the declaration `register regs[i < 4][j < 11];` defines two index parameters, `i` and `j`. In this case, the `indices` parameter is `[i, j]`.\\n\\n\\nThe `object` template provides the non-overridable method `cancel_after()`, which cancels all pending events posted using `after` which are associated with the object (any events associated with subobjects are unaffected).\\nThere are no other methods common for all objects, but the methods `init`, `post_init`, and `destroy` are automatically called on all objects that implement the [`init`](#init), [`post_init`](#post_init), and [`destroy`](#destroy) template, respectively.\\n## [4.2 Device objects](#device-objects)\\nThe top-level scope of a DML file defines the _device object_ , defined by the template `device`. The template provides an easy way to send memory transactions to objects that can be mapped into Simics memory maps. It defines a default implementation of `set` which assigns the session variable `map_target` of type `map_target_t *`, which can be used to issue transactions to the connected object. It also defines a default implementation of `validate` which verifies that the object can be used to create a map target, i.e. the Simics API `SIM_new_map_target` returns a valid pointer.\\nThe template defines the following methods:\\n  * `read(uint64 addr, uint64 size) -> (uint64) throws`\\nReads `size` bytes starting at `addr` in the connected object. Size must be 8 or less. Byte order is little-endian. Throws an exception if the read fails.\\n  * `read_bytes(uint64 addr, uint64 size, uint8 *bytes) throws`\\nReads `size` bytes into `bytes`, starting at `addr` in the connected object. The current state of the signal is stored in the saved boolean `high`, and a spec-violation message is logged on level 2 if the signal is raised or lowered when already high or low. The methods `signal_raise` and `signal_lower` can be overridden to add additional side effects.\\n### [5.5.2 signal_connect](#signal_connect)\\nImplements a connect with a signal interface, with saved state. The current state of the signal is stored in the saved boolean `signal.high`. If the connect is changed while `signal.high` is `true` and the device is configured, the `signal_lower` method will be called on the old object, and the `signal_raise` method will be called on the new object. Similarly, if the device is created with `signal.high` set to `true`, the `signal_raise` method will be called on the connected object in the finalize phase. This behaviour can be changed by overriding the `set` method and/or the `post_init` method. The template defines the following method:\\n  * `set_level(uint1 high)`: Sets the level of the signal, by calling `signal_raise` or `signal_lower`, as required. This parameter is typically used in log messages and names of configuration attributes. The name can be overridden in order to hide confidential information from the end-user.\\n### [4.1.2 desc](#desc)\\nProvides a string parameter `desc`, with a short description in plain text. By convention, this should preferably be a few descriptive words, but may also be a long name. The description can appear during simulation, when inspecting the device, and also serves as a docstring that enriches the DML source code. The parameter\'s default value is `undefined`. The `desc` parameter has a short-hand syntax described in section [Object Declarations](language.html#object-declarations).\\nAlso provides a string parameter `shown_desc`, which is the string actually exposed to the end-user during simulation. This parameter defaults to `desc`, and can be overridden in order to hide confidential information from the end-user.\\nSee also [template `documentation`](#documentation).\\n### [4.1.3 shown_desc](#shown_desc)\\nA subtemplate of [`desc`](#desc) that makes the `shown_desc` parameter a typed parameter. This is inherited by objects that need to access `shown_desc` from the context of a shared method.\\n### [4.1.4 documentation](#documentation)\\nProvides a string parameter `documentation`, with a longer description. The documentation may appear when extracting documentation from the device.\\nIf you have the _Documentation and Packaging_ package and intend to generate Simics reference documentation for the device then the `documentation` string must follow the Simics documentation XML format, otherwise you will get a syntax error during the documentation build. See the _Writing Documentation_ application note.\\nAlso provides a string parameter `shown_documentation`, defaulting to `documentation`. This parameter is similar to `shown_desc` in the [desc](#desc) template, and is mainly a convenient way to suppress documentation.\\n### [4.1.5 limitations](#limitations)\\nProvides a string parameter `limitations`, describing limitations in the implementation of this object. Built-in templates can be categorized as follows:\\n  * Each object type has a corresponding template which is instantiated for all objects of that type. For instance, the template `register` is automatically instantiated in all registers. All such templates inherit the `object` template, and define the `objtype` parameter to the name of the object type, e.g. `\\"register\\"` for registers.\\n  * Some templates primarily provide a standard implementation of some behaviour. For instance, the `uint64_attr` template can be applied on `attribute` objects to make it a simple integer attribute.\\n  * Some templates primarily specify a programming interface, typically by providing an abstract or overrideable method or parameter. Such templates are often named like the provided member. For instance, objects that implement the `init` template provide the abstract method `init`. Interface templates have a number of uses:\\n    * In some cases, an interface template extends an existing template, altering its default behaviour to make sure its interface method is called. Often, this means that when you provide an implementation of an interface method, you _must_ instantiate the corresponding template; otherwise, the method will not be called. For instance, if you implement the `write` method in a register, it is not called by default upon a write access; however, if you instantiate the `write` template in the register, then the register\'s behaviour is altered to call the `write` method. Thus, in order to provide custom side-effects on a write, you must write something like:\\n```\\nregister r @ 0 is write { method write(uint64 value) { default();\\nlog info: \\"wrote r\\"; } }\\n\\n```\\n\\n    * When writing a method or parameter override _inside a template_ , you must explicitly instantiate the template you are overriding in order to specify that your declaration takes precedence over the default implementation. If your template is intended for a specific object type, then it is sufficient to override that template, but it is often better to override a more specific template if possible. For instance, the `init_val` parameter belongs to the `init_val` template, which is inherited by all registers. So a template that overrides this parameter may be implemented as follows:\\n```\\ntemplate init_to_ten is register {\\n  param init_val = 10;\\n}\\n\\n```\\n\\nHowever, it is even better to only inherit the `init_val` template:\\n```\\ntemplate init_to_ten is init_val {\\n  param init_val = 10;\\n}\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe latter improves efficiency and permits `init_to_ten` to also be used on fields.\\n    * Similarly, if you write a template that needs to _access_ some member of the object, then it must inherit a template that provides that member. For instance:\\n```\\ntemplate log_on_change is (write, get, name) {\\n  method write(uint64 value) {\\n    if (this.get() != value) {\\n      log info: \\"%s changed!\\", this.name;\\n    }\\n    default();\\n  }\\n}",\n      "metadata": {\n        "url": "https://intel.github.io/simics/docs/dml-1.4-reference-manual/dml-builtins.html",\n        "source": "intel.github.io",\n        "headers": "# [4 Libraries and Built-ins](#libraries-and-built-ins)",\n        "source_id": "intel.github.io",\n        "char_count": 3213,\n        "chunk_size": 3213,\n        "local_file": "pipeline_output/downloaded_pages/simics_docs_dml-1.4-reference-manual_dml-builtins.html",\n        "word_count": 488,\n        "chunk_index": 0\n      },\n      "similarity": "NaN",\n      "source_id": "intel.github.io",\n      "rerank_score": -7.506108283996582\n    },\n    {\n      "url": "https://intel.github.io/simics/docs/dml-1.4-reference-manual/changes.html",\n      "content": "[C Managing deprecated language features](deprecations-auto.html) [D.4 Porting DML 1.2 to DML 1.4](port-dml.html)\\n[Device Modeling Language 1.4 Reference Manual](index.html) / \\n# [D Changes from DML 1.2 to DML 1.4](#changes-from-dml-1-2-to-dml-1-4)\\nDML 1.4 is in general very similar to DML 1.2 in syntax and semantics. However, some key differences do exist which we will highlight here.\\n## [D.1 Toy DML 1.2 device](#toy-dml-1-2-device)\\n```\\ndml 1.2;\\ndevice example;\\nmethod foo(a) -> (int b, bool c) nothrow {\\n  b = a;\\n  c = false;\\n  if (a == 0) {\\n    c = true;\\n  }\\n}\\nmethod bar(int a) {\\n  if (a == 0) {\\n    throw;\\n  }\\n}\\n// Bank\\nbank b {\\n  register r[20] size 4 @ 0x0000 + $i * 4 is read {\\n    method after_read(memop) {\\n      local int b;\\n      inline $foo(2) -> (b, $c);\\n    }\\n    field f [7:0];\\n    data bool c;\\n  }\\n}\\ntemplate t1 {\\n  parameter p default 1;\\n}\\ntemplate t2 {\\n  parameter p = 2;\\n}\\nattribute a is (t1, t2) {\\n  parameter allocate_type = \\"int32\\";\\n  method after_set() {\\n    call $bar($this);\\n    $this = $p;\\n int b;\\n  local bool c;\\n  b = a;\\n  c = false;\\n  #if (a == 0) {\\n    c = true;\\n  }\\n  return (b, c);\\n}\\nmethod bar(int a) throws {\\n  if (a == 0) {\\n    throw;\\n  }\\n}\\n// Bank\\nbank b {\\n  register r[i < 20] size 4 @ 0x0000 + i * 4 is read_register {\\n    method read_register(uint64 enabled_bytes, void *aux) -> (uint64) {\\n      local uint64 value = 1;\\n}\\ntemplate t2 is t1 {\\n  param p = 2;\\n}\\nattribute a is (int64_attr, t1, t2) {\\n  method set(attr_value_t value) throws {\\n    default(value);\\n    this.val = p;\\n  }\\n}\\n\\n```\\n\\n## [D.3 Key Differences](#key-differences)\\n```\\ndml 1.4;\\n\\n```\\n\\nIn very DML 1.4 file, this must be the first declaration.\\n```\\ninline method foo(inline a) ...\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe syntax for [inlining a method](language.html#inline-methods) call has changed. It is now strictly an attribute of the method being _called_ , declaring itself to be inline and which arguments are inline.\\n```\\n... -> (int, bool) /* b, c */\\n\\n```\\n\\nReturn values are no longer named in [method declarations](language.html#methods-detailed), rather only their types need to be declared. This also means they are not inherently available as variables in the method body scope.\\n```\\nlocal int b;\\nlocal bool c;\\n\\n```\\n\\nPreviously, these variables were declared as return values. They are now declared as [locals](language.html#local-statements) instead.\\n```\\n#if (a == 0) {\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe [`#if`](language.html#if-else-statements) syntax is useful to do compile-time evaluation of constants, in DML 1.2 this would be done with regular `if`.\\n```\\nreturn (b, c);\\n\\n```\\n\\nIn DML 1.4 you must explicitly return the return values.\\n```\\nmethod bar(int a) throws\\n\\n```\\n\\nA method that might throw must be annotated with the [`throws`](language.html#input-parameters-and-return-values) keyword.\\n```\\nregister r[i < 20] ...\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe syntax for [object arrays](language.html#object-declarations) has changed. The index name can no longer be implicit, and the range syntax is now `[index < size]`.\\n```\\n.., @ 0x0000 + i * 4 ...\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe `$` prefix on variables has been removed. See [Backward Incompatible changes](changes-manual.html#dollar_changes) for the variable scope implications of this.\\n```\\n... is read_register {\\n  method read_register(uint64 enabled_bytes, void *aux) -> (uint64) {\\n      local uint64 value = default(enabled_bytes, aux);\\n      ...\\n  }\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe API for the [builtin library](dml-builtins.html) has changed. In this case the `after_read` method has been replaced, and instead the `read_register` method must be overridden in order to call its `default` implementation and then implement the after read logic.\\n```\\nfield f @ [7:0];\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe syntax for [field declarations](language.html#field-declarations) has changed, you must now specify an `@` before the bit range of the field.\\n```\\nsession bool c;\\n\\n```\\n\\nData declarations have been replaced with [session](language.html#session-variables) declarations.\\n```\\ntemplate t1 {\\n  param p default 1;\\n}\\ntemplate t2 is t1 {\\n  param p = 2;\\n}\\nattribute a is (int64_attr, t1, t2) {\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe parameter override behaviour has been made stricter. In the case where multiple declarations of a parameter conflict DML will not pick a particular declaration if it is the only non-default one, rather it will check the [template hierarchy](language.html#templates) to determine which declarations override which.\\n```\\nmethod set(attr_value_t value) throws {\\n    default(value);\\n\\n```\\n\\nSimilar to above for registers, the `after_set` method of attributes is no more. Instead the set method must be overridden and `default` called.\\n```\\nthis.val = p;",\n      "metadata": {\n        "url": "https://intel.github.io/simics/docs/dml-1.4-reference-manual/changes.html",\n        "source": "intel.github.io",\n        "headers": "# [D Changes from DML 1.2 to DML 1.4](#changes-from-dml-1-2-to-dml-1-4); ## [D.1 Toy DML 1.2 device](#toy-dml-1-2-device); ## [D.2 The corresponding DML 1.4 device](#the-corresponding-dml-1-4-device); ## [D.3 Key Differences](#key-differences)",\n        "source_id": "intel.github.io",\n        "char_count": 4927,\n        "chunk_size": 4927,\n        "local_file": "pipeline_output/downloaded_pages/simics_docs_dml-1.4-reference-manual_changes.html",\n        "word_count": 778,\n        "chunk_index": 0\n      },\n      "similarity": "NaN",\n      "source_id": "intel.github.io",\n      "rerank_score": -10.416216850280762\n    }\n  ]\n}'}], 'structuredContent': {'result': '{\n  "success": true,\n  "query": "Simics Model Builder device creation and structure patterns",\n  "source_type": "docs",\n  "search_mode": "hybrid",\n  "reranking_applied": true,\n  "match_count": 5,\n  "results_count": 5,\n  "results": [\n    {\n      "url": "https://intel.github.io/simics/docs/dml-1.4-reference-manual/dml-builtins.html",\n      "content": "```\\n\\nAgain, it would also work to inherit `register` instead of `get` and `name`, but at the cost of reduced flexibility and efficiency.\\n\\n\\n## [4.1 Universal templates](#universal-templates)\\nThe following templates are applicable to all object kinds:\\n### [4.1.1 name](#name)\\nProvides a string parameter `name`, containing the name of the object, as exposed to the end-user. This parameter is typically used in log messages and names of configuration attributes. The name can be overridden in order to hide confidential information from the end-user.\\n### [4.1.2 desc](#desc)\\nProvides a string parameter `desc`, with a short description in plain text. By convention, this should preferably be a few descriptive words, but may also be a long name. The description can appear during simulation, when inspecting the device, and also serves as a docstring that enriches the DML source code. The parameter\'s default value is `undefined`. The `desc` parameter has a short-hand syntax described in section [Object Declarations](language.html#object-declarations).\\nAlso provides a string parameter `shown_desc`, which is the string actually exposed to the end-user during simulation. This parameter defaults to `desc`, and can be overridden in order to hide confidential information from the end-user.\\nSee also [template `documentation`](#documentation).\\n### [4.1.3 shown_desc](#shown_desc)\\nA subtemplate of [`desc`](#desc) that makes the `shown_desc` parameter a typed parameter. This is inherited by objects that need to access `shown_desc` from the context of a shared method.\\n### [4.1.4 documentation](#documentation)\\nProvides a string parameter `documentation`, with a longer description. The documentation may appear when extracting documentation from the device.\\nIf you have the _Documentation and Packaging_ package and intend to generate Simics reference documentation for the device then the `documentation` string must follow the Simics documentation XML format, otherwise you will get a syntax error during the documentation build. See the _Writing Documentation_ application note.\\nAlso provides a string parameter `shown_documentation`, defaulting to `documentation`. This parameter is similar to `shown_desc` in the [desc](#desc) template, and is mainly a convenient way to suppress documentation.\\n### [4.1.5 limitations](#limitations)\\nProvides a string parameter `limitations`, describing limitations in the implementation of this object. The documentation may appear when extracting documentation from the device.\\nIf you have the _Documentation and Packaging_ package and intend to generate Simics reference documentation for the device then the `limitations` string must follow the Simics documentation XML format, otherwise you will get a syntax error during the documentation build. See the _Writing Documentation_ application note.\\nAlso provides a string parameter `shown_limitations`, defaulting to `limitations`. Typically used to initialize a default value, or to set up data structures.\\nThe method `init` is automatically called on all objects that implement the `init` template. The order in which `init` of objects are called is not defined, except that `init` of a particular object is guaranteed to be called before `init` of any of its parent objects. In particular, `init` of the device object will be called only after all other implementations of `init`.\\n### [4.1.7 post_init](#post_init)\\nProvides an abstract method `post_init`, which is called when the device is created, _after_ any attributes have been initialized. Typically used to establish connections to other devices, or to set up data structures that depend on configured attribute values.\\nThe method `post_init` is automatically called on all objects that implement the `post_init` template.\\n### [4.1.8 destroy](#destroy)\\nProvides an abstract method `destroy`, which is called when the device is being deleted. This typically amounts to invoking [`delete`](language.html#delete-statements) on any device state that has been dynamically allocated using [`new`](language.html#new-expressions).\\nThe method `destroy` is automatically called on all objects that implement the `destroy` template. The order in which `destroy` of objects are called is not defined, except that `destroy` of a particular object is guaranteed to be called before `destroy` of any of its parent objects. Inherits the templates [`name`](#name), [`desc`](#desc), [`documentation`](#documentation) and [`limitations`](#limitations). Provides the following additional parameters, which cannot be overridden:\\n  * `this` (reference): Always refers to the current object, i.e., the nearest enclosing object definition.\\n  * `objtype`: string constant describing the object type, e.g. `\\"register\\"`\\n  * `parent` (reference or `undefined`): Always refers to the parent (containing) object. Has the value `undefined` in the `device` object.\\n  * qname: The fully qualified name, including indices, such as `some_bank.r0`. Constructed from the `name` parameter. In the device object, this is equal to the `name` parameter.\\n  * dev: The top-level `device` object\\n  * templates: see [Template-Qualified Method Implementation Calls](language.html#template-qualified-method-implementation-calls).\\n  * indices: List of local indices for this object. For a non-array object, this is the empty list. In a register array of size N, it is a list with one element, a non-negative integer smaller than N. The parameter is _not_ cumulative across the object hierarchy, so for a single field inside a register array, the value is the empty list.\\n  * Each array has an _individual index parameter_ , to make it possible to refer to both inner and outer indexes when arrays are nested (cf. the `indices` parameter, above). The parameter name is specified in the array declaration; for instance, the declaration `register regs[i < 4][j < 11];` defines two index parameters, `i` and `j`. In this case, the `indices` parameter is `[i, j]`.\\n\\n\\nThe `object` template provides the non-overridable method `cancel_after()`, which cancels all pending events posted using `after` which are associated with the object (any events associated with subobjects are unaffected).\\nThere are no other methods common for all objects, but the methods `init`, `post_init`, and `destroy` are automatically called on all objects that implement the [`init`](#init), [`post_init`](#post_init), and [`destroy`](#destroy) template, respectively.\\n## [4.2 Device objects](#device-objects)\\nThe top-level scope of a DML file defines the _device object_ , defined by the template `device`. The current state of the signal is stored in the saved boolean `high`, and a spec-violation message is logged on level 2 if the signal is raised or lowered when already high or low. The methods `signal_raise` and `signal_lower` can be overridden to add additional side effects.\\n### [5.5.2 signal_connect](#signal_connect)\\nImplements a connect with a signal interface, with saved state. The current state of the signal is stored in the saved boolean `signal.high`. If the connect is changed while `signal.high` is `true` and the device is configured, the `signal_lower` method will be called on the old object, and the `signal_raise` method will be called on the new object. Similarly, if the device is created with `signal.high` set to `true`, the `signal_raise` method will be called on the connected object in the finalize phase. This behaviour can be changed by overriding the `set` method and/or the `post_init` method. The template defines the following method:\\n  * `set_level(uint1 high)`: Sets the level of the signal, by calling `signal_raise` or `signal_lower`, as required. Built-in templates can be categorized as follows:\\n  * Each object type has a corresponding template which is instantiated for all objects of that type. For instance, the template `register` is automatically instantiated in all registers. All such templates inherit the `object` template, and define the `objtype` parameter to the name of the object type, e.g. `\\"register\\"` for registers.\\n  * Some templates primarily provide a standard implementation of some behaviour. For instance, the `uint64_attr` template can be applied on `attribute` objects to make it a simple integer attribute.\\n  * Some templates primarily specify a programming interface, typically by providing an abstract or overrideable method or parameter. Such templates are often named like the provided member. For instance, objects that implement the `init` template provide the abstract method `init`. Interface templates have a number of uses:\\n    * In some cases, an interface template extends an existing template, altering its default behaviour to make sure its interface method is called. Often, this means that when you provide an implementation of an interface method, you _must_ instantiate the corresponding template; otherwise, the method will not be called. For instance, if you implement the `write` method in a register, it is not called by default upon a write access; however, if you instantiate the `write` template in the register, then the register\'s behaviour is altered to call the `write` method. Thus, in order to provide custom side-effects on a write, you must write something like:\\n```\\nregister r @ 0 is write { method write(uint64 value) { default();\\nlog info: \\"wrote r\\"; } }\\n\\n```\\n\\n    * When writing a method or parameter override _inside a template_ , you must explicitly instantiate the template you are overriding in order to specify that your declaration takes precedence over the default implementation. If your template is intended for a specific object type, then it is sufficient to override that template, but it is often better to override a more specific template if possible. For instance, the `init_val` parameter belongs to the `init_val` template, which is inherited by all registers. So a template that overrides this parameter may be implemented as follows:\\n```\\ntemplate init_to_ten is register {\\n  param init_val = 10;\\n}\\n\\n```\\n\\nHowever, it is even better to only inherit the `init_val` template:\\n```\\ntemplate init_to_ten is init_val {\\n  param init_val = 10;\\n}\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe latter improves efficiency and permits `init_to_ten` to also be used on fields.\\n    * Similarly, if you write a template that needs to _access_ some member of the object, then it must inherit a template that provides that member. For instance:\\n```\\ntemplate log_on_change is (write, get, name) {\\n  method write(uint64 value) {\\n    if (this.get() != value) {\\n      log info: \\"%s changed!\\", this.name;\\n    }\\n    default();\\n  }\\n}",\n      "metadata": {\n        "url": "https://intel.github.io/simics/docs/dml-1.4-reference-manual/dml-builtins.html",\n        "source": "intel.github.io",\n        "headers": "# [4 Libraries and Built-ins](#libraries-and-built-ins)",\n        "source_id": "intel.github.io",\n        "char_count": 3213,\n        "chunk_size": 3213,\n        "local_file": "pipeline_output/downloaded_pages/simics_docs_dml-1.4-reference-manual_dml-builtins.html",\n        "word_count": 488,\n        "chunk_index": 0\n      },\n      "similarity": "NaN",\n      "source_id": "intel.github.io",\n      "rerank_score": -7.506108283996582\n    },\n    {\n      "url": "https://intel.github.io/simics/docs/dml-1.4-reference-manual/changes.html",\n      "content": "[C Managing deprecated language features](deprecations-auto.html) [D.4 Porting DML 1.2 to DML 1.4](port-dml.html)\\n[Device Modeling Language 1.4 Reference Manual](index.html) / \\n# [D Changes from DML 1.2 to DML 1.4](#changes-from-dml-1-2-to-dml-1-4)\\nDML 1.4 is in general very similar to DML 1.2 in syntax and semantics. However, some key differences do exist which we will highlight here.\\n## [D.1 Toy DML 1.2 device](#toy-dml-1-2-device)\\n```\\ndml 1.2;\\ndevice example;\\nmethod foo(a) -> (int b, bool c) nothrow {\\n  b = a;\\n  c = false;\\n  if (a == 0) {\\n    c = true;\\n  }\\n}\\nmethod bar(int a) {\\n  if (a == 0) {\\n    throw;\\n  }\\n}\\n// Bank\\nbank b {\\n  register r[20] size 4 @ 0x0000 + $i * 4 is read {\\n    method after_read(memop) {\\n      local int b;\\n      inline $foo(2) -> (b, $c);\\n    }\\n    field f [7:0];\\n    data bool c;\\n  }\\n}\\ntemplate t1 {\\n  parameter p default 1;\\n}\\ntemplate t2 {\\n  parameter p = 2;\\n}\\nattribute a is (t1, t2) {\\n  parameter allocate_type = \\"int32\\";\\n  method after_set() {\\n    call $bar($this);\\n    $this = $p;\\n  }\\n}\\n\\n```\\n\\n## [D.2 The corresponding DML 1.4 device](#the-corresponding-dml-1-4-device)\\n```\\ndml 1.4;\\ndevice example;\\ninline method foo(inline a) -> (int, bool) /* b, c */ {\\n  local int b;\\n  local bool c;\\n  b = a;\\n  c = false;\\n  #if (a == 0) {\\n    c = true;\\n  }\\n  return (b, c);\\n}\\nmethod bar(int a) throws {\\n  if (a == 0) {\\n    throw;\\n  }\\n}\\n// Bank\\nbank b {\\n  register r[i < 20] size 4 @ 0x0000 + i * 4 is read_register {\\n    method read_register(uint64 enabled_bytes, void *aux) -> (uint64) {\\n      local uint64 value = default(enabled_bytes, aux);\\n      local int b;\\n      (b, c) = foo(2);\\n      return value;\\n    }\\n    field f @ [7:0];\\n    session bool c;\\n  }\\n}\\ntemplate t1 {\\n  param p default 1;\\n}\\ntemplate t2 is t1 {\\n  param p = 2;\\n}\\nattribute a is (int64_attr, t1, t2) {\\n  method set(attr_value_t value) throws {\\n    default(value);\\n    this.val = p;\\n  }\\n}\\n\\n```\\n\\n## [D.3 Key Differences](#key-differences)\\n```\\ndml 1.4;\\n\\n```\\n\\nIn very DML 1.4 file, this must be the first declaration.\\n```\\ninline method foo(inline a) ...\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe syntax for [inlining a method](language.html#inline-methods) call has changed. It is now strictly an attribute of the method being _called_ , declaring itself to be inline and which arguments are inline.\\n```\\n... -> (int, bool) /* b, c */\\n\\n```\\n\\nReturn values are no longer named in [method declarations](language.html#methods-detailed), rather only their types need to be declared. This also means they are not inherently available as variables in the method body scope.\\n```\\nlocal int b;\\nlocal bool c;\\n\\n```\\n\\nPreviously, these variables were declared as return values. They are now declared as [locals](language.html#local-statements) instead.\\n```\\n#if (a == 0) {\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe [`#if`](language.html#if-else-statements) syntax is useful to do compile-time evaluation of constants, in DML 1.2 this would be done with regular `if`.\\n```\\nreturn (b, c);\\n\\n```\\n\\nIn DML 1.4 you must explicitly return the return values.\\n```\\nmethod bar(int a) throws\\n\\n```\\n\\nA method that might throw must be annotated with the [`throws`](language.html#input-parameters-and-return-values) keyword.\\n```\\nregister r[i < 20] ...\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe syntax for [object arrays](language.html#object-declarations) has changed. The index name can no longer be implicit, and the range syntax is now `[index < size]`.\\n```\\n.., @ 0x0000 + i * 4 ...\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe `$` prefix on variables has been removed. See [Backward Incompatible changes](changes-manual.html#dollar_changes) for the variable scope implications of this.\\n```\\n... is read_register {\\n  method read_register(uint64 enabled_bytes, void *aux) -> (uint64) {\\n      local uint64 value = default(enabled_bytes, aux);\\n      ...\\n  }\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe API for the [builtin library](dml-builtins.html) has changed. In this case the `after_read` method has been replaced, and instead the `read_register` method must be overridden in order to call its `default` implementation and then implement the after read logic.\\n```\\nfield f @ [7:0];\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe syntax for [field declarations](language.html#field-declarations) has changed, you must now specify an `@` before the bit range of the field.\\n```\\nsession bool c;\\n\\n```\\n\\nData declarations have been replaced with [session](language.html#session-variables) declarations.\\n```\\ntemplate t1 {\\n  param p default 1;\\n}\\ntemplate t2 is t1 {\\n  param p = 2;\\n}\\nattribute a is (int64_attr, t1, t2) {\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe parameter override behaviour has been made stricter. In the case where multiple declarations of a parameter conflict DML will not pick a particular declaration if it is the only non-default one, rather it will check the [template hierarchy](language.html#templates) to determine which declarations override which.\\n```\\nmethod set(attr_value_t value) throws {\\n    default(value);\\n\\n```\\n\\nSimilar to above for registers, the `after_set` method of attributes is no more. Instead the set method must be overridden and `default` called.\\n```\\nthis.val = p;",\n      "metadata": {\n        "url": "https://intel.github.io/simics/docs/dml-1.4-reference-manual/changes.html",\n        "source": "intel.github.io",\n        "headers": "# [D Changes from DML 1.2 to DML 1.4](#changes-from-dml-1-2-to-dml-1-4); ## [D.1 Toy DML 1.2 device](#toy-dml-1-2-device); ## [D.2 The corresponding DML 1.4 device](#the-corresponding-dml-1-4-device); ## [D.3 Key Differences](#key-differences)",\n        "source_id": "intel.github.io",\n        "char_count": 4927,\n        "chunk_size": 4927,\n        "local_file": "pipeline_output/downloaded_pages/simics_docs_dml-1.4-reference-manual_changes.html",\n        "word_count": 778,\n        "chunk_index": 0\n      },\n      "similarity": "NaN",\n      "source_id": "intel.github.io",\n      "rerank_score": -10.416216850280762\n    }\n  ]\n}'}, 'isError': False}

In 1.2, you overrode the access() method.  In\\n// DML 1.4, it makes more sense to override each specific type\\n// of operation.\\n//\\n// size_from_enabled: \\n//          turn a byte mask (assuming packed bits to the right)  \\n//          which seems to be safe for this purpose, even though\\n//          there are cases where an arbitrary byte enable mask\\n//          is being used. \\n//\\nmethod size_from_enabled(uint64 enabled_bytes) -> (uint64) {\\n   local uint64 sz = 0;\\n   if(enabled_bytes >= 0xFFFFFFFF) {\\n       enabled_bytes >>= 32;\\n       sz+=4;\\n   }\\n   if(enabled_bytes >= 0xFFFF) {\\n       enabled_bytes >>= 16;\\n       sz+=2;\\n   }\\n   if(enabled_bytes >= 0xFF) {\\n       enabled_bytes >>= 8;\\n       sz+=1;\\n   }\\n   if(enabled_bytes >= 0xFF) {\\n       sz+=1;\\n   }   \\n   return sz; \\n    }\\n\\n    // Read is called for actual read operations\\n    method read(uint64 offset, uint64 enabled_bytes, void *aux) -> (uint64) throws {\\n        local uint64 value = 0xc001d00ddeadbeef & enabled_bytes;\\n        log info, 1: \\"Read access to offset 0x%llx, size=0x%llx -> read value=0x%llx\\", \\n                     offset, size_from_enabled(enabled_bytes), value; Posting event in %d ps\\", ps;\\n            log info, 4: \\"                      Which is %f s\\", secs;\\n            after secs s: tick();\\n            // Once DML supports ps posting in after, switch to ps \\n            // and remove the conversion to seconds. \\n            return;\\n        }\\n\\n        // Default: post using cycles \\n        local uint64 t = regs.event_interval.val;\\n        if(t==0) {\\n            log spec_viol, 1: \\"Callback interval of zero is ignored.\\";\\n        } else {\\n            log info, 3: \\"Posting event in %d cycles\\", t;\\n            after t cycles: tick();\\n        }\\n    }\\n\\n    method stop() {\\n        callback.cancel_after();\\n    }\\n\\n    method tick() {\\n      regs.event_counter.val += 1;\\n      log info, 2: \\"Tick (new counter value=%d)\\", regs.event_counter.val;\\n      SIM_notify(obj, ntfy_id);\\n      post_next();\\n    }    \\n}\\n\\nbank regs \\"software-visible programming registers\\" {\\n    register event_interval size 4 @ 0x0000 is (read, write) \\"Event Interval\\";\\n\\n    register start_stop     size 4 @ 0x0004 is (read, write) \\"Start!=0; It is, however, recommended that the following conventions are followed in order to make it easy for a user to understand the purpose of a particular object:\\n  * `object.port`.<port>\\nObjects under the `port` namespace should be used for objects implementing interfaces on behalf of `object`.\\n  * `object.bank`.<bank>\\nThe `bank` namespace is used for objects implementing register banks on behalf of `object`.\\n  * `object`.<name>\\nUsed for all other kinds of objects.\\n\\n\\nThe `object.port` and `object.bank` objects are just plain namespaces. For specific objects, there might also be other namespaces that have been assigned a special meaning:\\n  * `cpu.vtime`.<clock>\\nAll CPUs have a `vtime` object which handles event dispatching. Below this objects there are objects implementing event queues used by the CPU. There are normally at least a `cycles` object which contains the cycle based event queue and a `ps` object which holds the pico-seconds based event queue.\\n\\n\\n## [34.5 Object Initialization](#object-hierarchy.html:object-initialization)\\nThe creation of Simics objects is a process which contains several distinct steps that are executed in a strict sequence. Each step is performed for all objects under construction before the next step is attempted. The sequence looks as follows:\\n  1. Space is allocated for all objects under construction by calling the `alloc` function from the corresponding class.\\n  2. The Simics private parts of the objects are initialized and the objects are added to the object hierarchy.\\n  3. The `init` method is called for each object.\\n  4. Object attributes of the kind `Sim_Init_Phase_Pre1` are set for all objects. Attributes of this kind should only be registered by classes provided by Simics Core.\\n  5. Normal attributes are set for all objects.\\n  6. Object attributes marked with `Sim_Init_Phase_1` are set for all objects.\\n  7. The `finalize` method is called for each object. The object is marked as configured.\\n  8. The `objects_finalized` method is called for each object.\\n\\n\\nThe object ordering for each step is defined by the object hierarchy, with parents being initialized before any descendants. Siblings are ordered alphanumerically. For the last step, there can be exceptions to this rule caused by explicit usage of the `SIM_require_object` function from the `finalize` method.\\nThe particulars of the object initialization sequence and initialization ordering have some implications:\\n  * An object can use the `SIM_object_descendant` function from its `init` method to obtain a reference to a child object. This is particularly useful for obtaining references to automatically created port objects.\\n  * A port object can obtain a reference to its port object parent from the `init` method by using the `SIM_port_object_parent` function.\\n  * Objects can propagate default attribute values to children using the `SIM_set_attribute_default` function. This can be done from the `init` function but also from individual attribute setters.",\n      "metadata": {\n        "url": "local://intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n        "source": "intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n        "headers": "## [34.4 Naming Conventions](#object-hierarchy.html:naming-conventions); ## [34.5 Object Initialization](#object-hierarchy.html:object-initialization)",\n        "source_id": "intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n        "char_count": 3133,\n        "chunk_size": 3133,\n        "local_file": "pipeline_output/downloaded_pages/intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n        "word_count": 462,\n        "chunk_index": 169\n      },\n      "similarity": "NaN",\n      "source_id": "intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n      "rerank_score": -10.896224975585938\n    },\n    {\n      "url": "local://intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n      "content": "```\\nclass gertie(StandardConnectorComponent):\\n  \\"\\"\\"The Gertie PCI component.\\"\\"\\"\\n  _class_desc = \\"a Gertie PCI component\\"\\n  _help_categories = (\'PCI\',)\\n  def setup(self):\\n    super().setup()\\n    if not self.instantiated.val:\\n      self.add_gertie_objects()\\n    self.add_gertie_connectors()\\n  def add_gertie_connectors(self):\\n    self.add_connector(\'pci\', PciBusUpConnector(0, \'sample_dev\'))\\n  def add_gertie_objects(self):\\n    self.add_pre_obj(\'sample_dev\', \'sample_pci_device\',\\n             int_attr = 10)\\n\\n```\\n\\nNote that connectors instantiating a helper connector class that handles the connection must instantiate the connector class even if the component has been instantiated. A component usually does not have to override the `component` interface.\\n### [26.6.13 Adding Dynamic Connectors](#creating-configurations.html:adding-dynamic-connectors)\\nA connector can support connecting to multiple connectors or just one other connector. A component that has connectors must implement the `component_connector` interface. Below we describe how to add connectors either by explicitly implementing the `component_connector` interface in section [26.6.10.1](#creating-configurations.html:defining-connector-explicitly), or using connector classes in section [26.6.10.2](#creating-configurations.html:connector-classes).\\n#### [26.6.10.1 Defining Connector Explicitly](#creating-configurations.html:defining-connector-explicitly)\\nOne way of implementing connectors is to define the `component_connector` interface and implement all the functions that are needed for the connectors.\\n```\\nclass elliot(StandardComponent):\\n  \\"\\"\\"The Elliot component.\\"\\"\\"\\n  _class_desc = \'an Elliot component\'\\n  def setup(self):\\n    super().setup()\\n    if not self.instantiated.val:\\n      self.add_elliot_objects()\\n  def add_elliot_objects(self):\\n    self.add_connector(\\n       \'eth0\', \'ethernet-link\', True, False, False,\\n       simics.Sim_Connector_Direction_Down)\\n    self.add_connector(\\n       \'uart[2]\', \'serial\', True, False, False,\\n       simics.Sim_Connector_Direction_Down)\\n    dbg = self.add_connector(\\n       None, \'serial\', True, False, False,\\n       simics.Sim_Connector_Direction_Down)\\n    self.add_slot(\'debug\', dbg)\\n  class component_connector(Interface):\\n    def get_check_data(self, cnt):\\n      # same as connect_data\\n      return self._up.get_connect_data(cnt)\\n    def get_connect_data(self, cnt):\\n      return self._up.get_connect_data(cnt)\\n    def check(self, cnt, attr):\\n      return True\\n    def connect(self, cnt, attr):\\n      self._up.connect(cnt, attr)\\n    def disconnect(self, cnt):\\n      self._up.disconnect(cnt)\\n  def get_connect_data(self, cnt):\\n    if cnt in self.get_slot(\'uart\'):\\n      num = self.get_slot(\'uart\').index(cnt)\\n      return [None, self.get_slot(\'uart_dev%d\' % num), \'uart%d\' % num]\\n    elif cnt == self.get_slot(\'debug\'):\\n      return [None, self.get_slot(\'dbg_dev\'), \'debug\']\\n    elif cnt.type == \'ethernet-link\':\\n      return []\\n  def connect(self, cnt, attr):\\n    if cnt in self.get_slot(\'uart\'):\\n      (link, console) = attr\\n      num = self.get_slot(\'uart\').index(cnt)\\n      self.get_slot(\'uart_dev%d\' % num).console = console\\n    elif cnt == self.get_slot(\'debug\'):\\n      (link, console) = attr\\n      self.get_slot(\'dbg_dev\').console = console\\n    elif cnt == self.get_slot(\'eth0\'):\\n      self.get_slot(\'emac0\').link = attr[0]\\n  def disconnect(self, cnt):\\n    if cnt in self.get_slot(\'uart\'):\\n      num = self.get_slot(\'uart\').index(cnt)\\n      self.get_slot(\'uart_dev%d\' % num).console = None\\n    elif cnt == self.get_slot(\'debug\'):\\n      self.get_slot(\'dbg_dev\').console = None\\n    elif cnt == self.get_slot(\'eth0\'):\\n      self.get_slot(\'emac0\').link = None\\n\\n```\\n\\nThe example component creates one connector in the slot _eth0_ , one array of two connectors in the slot _uart_ , and one connector in the slot _debug_.\\nThe connector objects are created at once when adding a connector slot with the `add_connector` method. The function returns the objects or nested arrays of objects if the slot was specified, otherwise the function returns pre objects or nested arrays of pre objects, like the _dbg_ connector in the example. In the latter case the objects are created when they are assigned to a slot using the `add_slot` function. This is to avoid creating connectors that are never assigned to any slot and therefore are useless.\\nThe component implements the `component_connector` interface to handle the connector functionality. The interface is documented in the _API Reference Manual_. The different connector types are documented in section [26.10](#creating-configurations.html:standard-connector-types).\\n#### [26.6.10.2 Connector Classes](#creating-configurations.html:connector-classes)\\nMost connectors are simple connectors with standard behavior. This makes it possible to use the same code for several components. The `StandardConnectorComponent` class in the `comp` Python module helps with this. The `StandardConnectorComponent` class inherits the `StandardComponent` class and supports the same features as that class. In addition the `StandardConnectorComponent` class implements the `component_connector` interface and a new definition of the `add_connector` method. The function takes a _connector class_ as argument. The _connector class_ provides all functionality for handling the connection.\\nThe most common standard type of connectors has predefined connector classes. They are included in the `connector` Python module. This module is imported by the `comp` module, so there is no need to import it explicitly. The source code for these classes can be found in `[simics]/src/core/common/connectors.py`.",\n      "metadata": {\n        "url": "local://intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n        "source": "intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n        "headers": "### [26.6.10 Adding Connectors](#creating-configurations.html:adding-connectors); #### [26.6.10.1 Defining Connector Explicitly](#creating-configurations.html:defining-connector-explicitly); #### [26.6.10.2 Connector Classes](#creating-configurations.html:connector-classes)",\n        "source_id": "intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n        "char_count": 4950,\n        "chunk_size": 4950,\n        "local_file": "pipeline_output/downloaded_pages/intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n        "word_count": 533,\n        "chunk_index": 148\n      },\n      "similarity": "NaN",\n      "source_id": "intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n      "rerank_score": -11.335161209106445\n    }\n  ]\n}'}], 'structuredContent': {'result': '{\n  "success": true,\n  "query": "ARM PrimeCell watchdog timer specification register map",\n  "source_type": "docs",\n  "search_mode": "hybrid",\n  "reranking_applied": true,\n  "match_count": 5,\n  "results_count": 5,\n  "results": [\n    {\n      "url": "local://intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n      "content": "```\\n\\n## [34.4 Naming Conventions](#object-hierarchy.html:naming-conventions)\\nSimics does not impose any rules how child objects should be named. It is, however, recommended that the following conventions are followed in order to make it easy for a user to understand the purpose of a particular object:\\n  * `object.port`.<port>\\nObjects under the `port` namespace should be used for objects implementing interfaces on behalf of `object`.\\n  * `object.bank`.<bank>\\nThe `bank` namespace is used for objects implementing register banks on behalf of `object`.\\n  * `object`.<name>\\nUsed for all other kinds of objects.\\n\\n\\nThe `object.port` and `object.bank` objects are just plain namespaces. For specific objects, there might also be other namespaces that have been assigned a special meaning:\\n  * `cpu.vtime`.<clock>\\nAll CPUs have a `vtime` object which handles event dispatching. Below this objects there are objects implementing event queues used by the CPU. There are normally at least a `cycles` object which contains the cycle based event queue and a `ps` object which holds the pico-seconds based event queue.\\n\\n\\n## [34.5 Object Initialization](#object-hierarchy.html:object-initialization)\\nThe creation of Simics objects is a process which contains several distinct steps that are executed in a strict sequence. Each step is performed for all objects under construction before the next step is attempted. The sequence looks as follows:\\n  1. Space is allocated for all objects under construction by calling the `alloc` function from the corresponding class.\\n  2. The Simics private parts of the objects are initialized and the objects are added to the object hierarchy.\\n  3. The `init` method is called for each object.\\n  4. Object attributes of the kind `Sim_Init_Phase_Pre1` are set for all objects. Attributes of this kind should only be registered by classes provided by Simics Core.\\n  5. Normal attributes are set for all objects.\\n  6. Object attributes marked with `Sim_Init_Phase_1` are set for all objects.\\n  7. The `finalize` method is called for each object. The object is marked as configured.\\n  8. The `objects_finalized` method is called for each object.\\n\\n\\nThe object ordering for each step is defined by the object hierarchy, with parents being initialized before any descendants. Siblings are ordered alphanumerically. For the last step, there can be exceptions to this rule caused by explicit usage of the `SIM_require_object` function from the `finalize` method.\\nThe particulars of the object initialization sequence and initialization ordering have some implications:\\n  * An object can use the `SIM_object_descendant` function from its `init` method to obtain a reference to a child object. This is particularly useful for obtaining references to automatically created port objects.\\n  * A port object can obtain a reference to its port object parent from the `init` method by using the `SIM_port_object_parent` function.\\n  * Objects can propagate default attribute values to children using the `SIM_set_attribute_default` function. This can be done from the `init` function but also from individual attribute setters.",\n      "metadata": {\n        "url": "local://intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n        "source": "intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n        "headers": "## [34.4 Naming Conventions](#object-hierarchy.html:naming-conventions); ## [34.5 Object Initialization](#object-hierarchy.html:object-initialization)",\n        "source_id": "intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n        "char_count": 3133,\n        "chunk_size": 3133,\n        "local_file": "pipeline_output/downloaded_pages/intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n        "word_count": 462,\n        "chunk_index": 169\n      },\n      "similarity": "NaN",\n      "source_id": "intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n      "rerank_score": -10.896224975585938\n    },\n    {\n      "url": "local://intel_github_io_indexsingle-page_dc7a2578.html",\n      "content": "```\\nclass gertie(StandardConnectorComponent):\\n  \\"\\"\\"The Gertie PCI component.\\"\\"\\"\\n  _class_desc = \\"a Gertie PCI component\\"\\n  _help_categories = (\'PCI\',)\\n  def setup(self):\\n    super().setup()\\n    if not self.instantiated.val:\\n      self.add_gertie_objects()\\n    self.add_gertie_connectors()\\n  def add_gertie_connectors(self):\\n    self.add_connector(\'pci\', PciBusUpConnector(0, \'sample_dev\'))\\n  def add_gertie_objects(self):\\n    self.add_pre_obj(\'sample_dev\', \'sample_pci_device\',\\n             int_attr = 10)\\n\\n```\\n\\nNote that connectors instantiating a helper connector class that handles the connection must instantiate the connector class even if the component has been instantiated. This kind of copy can only be done for connector objects. Note that this is a superior solution to the runtime command `copy-connector`, which has the same effect at run time but does not update the documentation and requires care when checkpointing.\\nNote that the `get_slot` and `copy_connector` methods can get slots not only in the components own namespace but in the sub components namespace. In the example this is done by the _self.get_slot(\'last.clock\')_ call. It is also possible to access slots in a sub component to the sub component. There is no limit to the look-up depth.\\n#### [26.6.11.2 Adding and Connecting Sub Components](#creating-configurations.html:adding-and-connecting-sub-components)\\nTwo sub components can be both The two sub components are connected using the `connect` method. Arguments to the method are the connectors in the sub components.\\n### [26.6.12 Overriding Interfaces](#creating-configurations.html:overriding-interfaces)\\nThe `StandardComponent` class implements the `component` interface. The interface implementation can be overridden if needed.\\n```\\nclass godzilla(StandardComponent):\\n  \\"\\"\\"The Godzilla component.\\"\\"\\"\\n  _class_desc = \'a Godzilla component\'\\n  def setup(self):\\n    super().setup()\\n    if not self.instantiated.val:\\n      self.add_godzilla_objects()\\n  def add_godzilla_objects(self):\\n    self.add_pre_obj(\'mem\', \'memory-space\')\\n    self.add_pre_obj(\'p_mem\', \'memory-space\')\\n  class component(StandardComponent.component):\\n    def post_instantiate(self):\\n      self._up.get_slot(\'mem\').default_target = [\\n        self._up.get_slot(\'p_mem\'), 0, 0, None]\\n\\n```\\n\\nIn the example we override the `post_instantiate` method. A component usually does not have to override the `component` interface.\\n### [26.6.13 Adding Dynamic Connectors](#creating-configurations.html:adding-dynamic-connectors)\\nA connector can support connecting to multiple connectors or just one other connector.
